Split sessions into 60-second trials.\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time elapsed from the beginning of the session in seconds. Time-varying.\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Motion energy, discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session. list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"longitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`.\n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\nDecode information regarding animal motion from the neural activities recorded from mouse barrel cortex. Split sessions into 60-second trials.\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time elapsed from the beginning of the session in seconds. Time-varying.\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Motion energy, discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session. Time-varying.\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Dropped frames are detected by interframe intervals exceeding 0.04s and are filled by inserting the average of neighboring values. After interpolation, an assertion verifies the lengths match. Since trials are contiguous 60-second segments of the continuous recording, no event-based alignment is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\n'metadata': {\n    'temporal_alignment_event': 'session_start',\n    'off_start': None,\n    'off_end': None,\n}\n```\n\niii. There is no stimulus event to align to. The recording is continuous, and trials are artificial segments starting from the beginning of the session.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. Both the neural and the motion energy traces are averaged into non-overlapping bins of 10 consecutive frames, taking 30 Hz to 3 Hz, i.e. a 333.33 ms time bin. Binning is applied before the motion energy is discretized.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_FRAMES = 10\n...\nFc = bin_frames(Fc)\nme = bin_frames(me)\n...\n'metadata': {\n    'time_bin_size': BIN_FRAMES / FS * 1000,  # ms\n}\n```\n\niii. The Methods state \"for all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\". Both streams are binned together so they stay the same length, and this has to precede discretization because averaging class labels would be meaningless.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time* derived from?\n\ni. Time is not derived from any raw data variable. It is computed from the time bin index and the bin duration, giving **seconds from the start of that session**. Bins are 10 frames at 30 Hz, so the step is 1/3 s and a 30-minute session spans [0.0, 1799.67]. The value is the left edge of each bin and runs continuously across trials within a session. It is named `time_from_session_start`.\n\nii.\n```python\nt = ((s + np.arange(trial_frames)) * BIN_FRAMES / FS).astype(np.float32)\ninp_trials.append(t[np.newaxis, :])  # (1, trial_frames)\n```\n\niii. Since the frame rate is constant at 30 Hz and there are no timestamps stored with the data, computing time from bin indices is equivalent.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time*?\n\nN/A\n\n## 3-c. How is `input` *Time* aligned with the neural data?\n\nN/A\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Motion energy* derived from?\n\ni. Motion energy is derived from `motion_energy_glob.npy` in the `move_deve` subdirectory of each session. Interframe intervals from `interframe_int.npy` are used to detect and interpolate dropped frames.\n\nii.\n```python\nme = np.load(os.path.join(session_path, 'move_deve', 'motion_energy_glob.npy'))\ndt = np.load(os.path.join(session_path, 'move_deve', 'interframe_int.npy'))\n```\n\niii. The motion energy file contains a pre-computed global motion energy signal from the behavioral video. The interframe interval file is needed to identify dropped video frames that must be interpolated to match the neural data length.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Motion energy*?\n\ni. Three processing steps: (1) dropped frames are detected via interframe intervals >0.04 s and interpolated by averaging neighboring values, (2) the trace is averaged into 10-frame bins along with the neural data, (3) the binned signal is discretized into 5 percentile-based bins whose edges are computed **within each session**. \n\nii.\n```python\nif me.shape[0] < expected_len:\n    drop_indices = np.where(dt * 1000 > 0.04)[0]\n    for offset, idx in enumerate(drop_indices):\n        insert_pos = idx + 1 + offset\n        interp_val = (me[insert_pos - 1] + me[insert_pos]) / 2.0\n        me = np.insert(me, insert_pos, interp_val)\n...\nme = bin_frames(me)\n...\npercentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_levels + 1)\nfor me in all_me_flat:\n    bin_edges = np.percentile(me, percentiles)   # session-local edges\n    output = np.digitize(me, bin_edges[1:-1])    # levels 0 .. n_levels-1\n```\n\niii. Dropped frame interpolation ensures the motion energy signal matches the neural data length frame-for-frame. \n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The video and neural data are acquired synchronously at 30 Hz, so they are aligned frame-for-frame in principle. However, occasional video frames are dropped, making the motion energy array shorter than the neural data. Dropped frames are detected by interframe intervals exceeding 0.04s and are filled by inserting the average of neighboring values. After interpolation, an assertion verifies the lengths match. The time unit here is a bit strange but `dt * 1000 > 0.04` seems to work well.\n\nii.\n```python\nme = preprocess_motion_energy(session_path, expected_len=Fc.shape[1])\n...\nif me.shape[0] < expected_len:\n    drop_indices = np.where(dt * 1000 > 0.04)[0]\n    n_missing = expected_len - me.shape[0]\n    for offset, idx in enumerate(drop_indices):\n        insert_pos = idx + 1 + offset\n        interp_val = (me[insert_pos - 1] + me[insert_pos]) / 2.0\n        me = np.insert(me, insert_pos, interp_val)\n\nassert me.shape[0] == expected_len\n...\nneural_trials.append(Fc[:, s:e])\noutput_trials.append(out[np.newaxis, s:e])\n```\n\niii. The interframe interval threshold of 0.04s (slightly above the expected 1/30 ≈ 0.033s interval) identifies frames where the video missed a capture. Linear interpolation fills these gaps so that both streams can be indexed identically.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Dropped video frames are detected and interpolated (see 4-c). An assertion verifies the motion energy length matches the neural data length after interpolation. Remainder frames at the end of a session that don't fill a complete trial are discarded.\n\nii.\n```python\nassert me.shape[0] == expected_len, (\n    f'motion energy length {me.shape[0]} != expected {expected_len}'\n)\n...\nif remainder > 0:\n    print(f'  session {idx}: discarding last {remainder} frames '\n          f'({remainder/FS:.1f}s) that do not fill a full trial')\n```\n\niii. The assertion ensures any frame count mismatch is caught rather than silently producing misaligned data. Discarding remainder frames is a minor data loss (at most 59 seconds per session).\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming step is the suite2p `dcnv.preprocess` baseline correction, which runs on GPU. Loading the `.npy` files is also I/O bound but relatively fast.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The baseline correction involves sliding window operations over the full session length for every neuron. GPU acceleration (`DEVICE = torch.device('cuda')`) mitigates this.\n\n## 6-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. This could be vectorized by pre-allocating the output array and filling in all interpolated values at once.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. f'motion energy length {me.shape[0]} != expected {expected_len}'\n    )\n\n    print(f'    ME: range [{me.min():.4f}, {me.max():.4f}]  mean={me.mean():.4f}  std={me.std():.4f}')\n    return Layer 2/3 of barrel cortex\n(S1), 720x720 um FOV, resonant scanner at ~30 Hz, sessions of 20 min (36000\nframes) or 30 min (54000 frames). neucoeff * Fneu\n    win = int(win_baseline * fs)\n    Flow = gaussian_filter(Fc, [0., sig_baseline])\n    Flow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n    Flow = maximum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n    return Fc - Flow\n\n\ndef camera_frame_index(tstamps, n_frames):\n    \"\"\"Map each recorded camera frame onto its imaging-frame index.\n\n    The camera was triggered by the microscope, so nominally camera frame i is\n    imaging frame i.  When the camera drops frames the timestamp trace shows\n    gaps that are exact multiples of the frame interval; the number of dropped\n    frames in each gap recovers the true index.\n    \"\"\"\n    ncam = len(tstamps)\n    if ncam == n_frames:\n        # nothing dropped\n        return np.arange(n_frames)\n    ifi = np.diff(tstamps)\n    dt = np.median(ifi)\n    n_missing = np.round(ifi / dt).astype(int) - 1\n    idx = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(1 + n_missing)])\n    assert idx[-1] == n_frames - 1, 'could not reconstruct dropped camera frames'\n    assert n_missing.sum() == n_frames - ncam\n    return idx\n\n\ndef interp_nan(x):\n    \"\"\"Linear interpolation over NaNs (edges filled with nearest valid value).\"\"\"\n    x = np.asarray(x, dtype=np.float64)\n    bad = np.isnan(x)\n    if bad.all():\n        raise ValueError('all values missing')\n    t = np.arange(len(x))\n    x[bad] = np.interp(t[bad], t[~bad], x[~bad])\n    return x\n\n\ndef bin_mean(x, k):\n    \"\"\"Average non-overlapping bins of k samples along the last axis.\"\"\"\n    n = (x.shape[-1] // k) * k\n    x = x[..., :n]\n    return x.reshape(*x.shape[:-1], n // k, k).mean(axis=-1)\n\n\ndef load_session(session_dir):\n    \"\"\"Return binned dF/F (n_neurons, n_bins), bin time in s, binned motion energy.\"\"\"\n    s2p = os.path.join(session_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0')\n    mov = os.path.join(session_dir, 'move_deve')\n\n    F = np.load(os.path.join(s2p, 'F.npy'))\n    Fneu = np.load(os.path.join(s2p, 'Fneu.npy'))\n    ops = np.load(os.path.join(s2p, 'ops.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\n    iscell = np.load(os.path.join(s2p, 'iscell.npy'))\n    fs = float(ops['fs'])\n    n_frames = F.shape[1]\n\n    # neucoeff * Fneu\n    win = int(win_baseline * fs)\n    Flow = gaussian_filter(Fc, [0., sig_baseline])\n    Flow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n    Flow = maximum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n    return Fc - Flow\n\n\ndef camera_frame_index(tstamps, n_frames):\n    \"\"\"Map each recorded camera frame onto its imaging-frame index.\n\n    The camera was triggered by the microscope, so nominally camera frame i is\n    imaging frame i.  When the camera drops frames the timestamp trace shows\n    gaps that are exact multiples of the frame interval; the number of dropped\n    frames in each gap recovers the true index.\n    \"\"\"\n    ncam = len(tstamps)\n    if ncam == n_frames:\n        # nothing dropped\n        return np.arange(n_frames)\n    ifi = np.diff(tstamps)\n    dt = np.median(ifi)\n    n_missing = np.round(ifi / dt).astype(int) - 1\n    idx = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(1 + n_missing)])\n    assert idx[-1] == n_frames - 1, 'could not reconstruct dropped camera frames'\n    assert n_missing.sum() == n_frames - ncam\n    return idx\n\n\ndef interp_nan(x):\n    \"\"\"Linear interpolation over NaNs (edges filled with nearest valid value).\"\"\"\n    x = np.asarray(x, dtype=np.float64)\n    bad = np.isnan(x)\n    if bad.all():\n        raise ValueError('all values missing')\n    t = np.arange(len(x))\n    x[bad] = np.interp(t[bad], t[~bad], x[~bad])\n    return x\n\n\ndef bin_mean(x, k):\n    \"\"\"Average non-overlapping bins of k samples along the last axis.\"\"\"\n    n = (x.shape[-1] // k) * k\n    x = x[..., :n]\n    return x.reshape(*x.shape[:-1], n // k, k).mean(axis=-1)\n\n\ndef load_session(session_dir):\n    \"\"\"Return binned dF/F (n_neurons, n_bins), bin time in s, binned motion energy.\"\"\"\n    s2p = os.path.join(session_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0')\n    mov = os.path.join(session_dir, 'move_deve')\n\n    F = np.load(os.path.join(s2p, 'F.npy'))\n    Fneu = np.load(os.path.join(s2p, 'Fneu.npy'))\n    ops = np.load(os.path.join(s2p, 'ops.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\n    iscell = np.load(os.path.join(s2p, 'iscell.npy'))\n    fs = float(ops['fs'])\n    n_frames = F.shape[1]\n\n    # The released Track2p output only contains tracked cells and they all pass\n    # suite2p's default iscell threshold of 0.5; assert rather than filter.\n    assert np.all(iscell[:, 0] == 1), 'unexpected non-cell ROI in Track2p output'\n\n    dff = f_processing(F.astype(np.float64), Fneu.astype(np.float64), fs)\n\n    # --- behaviour, put back onto the imaging frame grid -------------------\n    me_cam = np.load(os.path.join(mov, 'motion_energy_glob.npy')).astype(np.float64)\n    # tstamps are in units of 1000 s; convert to seconds\n    ts_cam = np.load(os.path.join(mov, 'tstamps.npy')).astype(np.float64) * 1000.0\n    assert len(me_cam) == len(ts_cam)\n\n    idx = camera_frame_index(ts_cam, n_frames)\n\n    me = np.full(n_frames, np.nan)\n    me[idx] = me_cam\n    # motion energy of the very first frame is 0 by construction (no preceding\n    # frame to difference against): treat it as missing\n    me[0] = np.nan\n    me = interp_nan(me)\n\n    tstamps = np.full(n_frames, np.nan)\n    tstamps[idx] = ts_cam\n    tstamps = interp_nan(tstamps)\n\n    # --- denoise both streams by averaging 10 consecutive frames -----------\n    dff_b = bin_mean(dff, FRAMES_PER_BIN)\n    me_b = bin_mean(me, FRAMES_PER_BIN)\n    t_b = bin_mean(tstamps, FRAMES_PER_BIN)  # bin-centre time, seconds\n\n    return dff_b.astype(np.float32), t_b, me_b, float(np.median(np.diff(tstamps)))\n\n\ndef discretize_quintiles(x, nbins=N_OUTPUT_BINS):\n    \"\"\"Assign each sample to one of `nbins` equal-percentile bins of x.\"\"\"\n    edges = np.percentile(x, np.linspace(0, 100, nbins + 1)[1:-1])\n    return np.digitize(x, edges).astype(np.int64)\n\n\ndef main():\n    subjects = sorted(d for d in os.listdir(DATA_DIR)\n                      if os.path.isdir(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, d)))\n\n    neural, inputs, outputs = [], [], []\n    subject_idx, brain_region_idx, session_info = [], [], []\n    bin_durations = []\n\n    for si, subj in enumerate(subjects):\n        subj_dir = os.path.join(DATA_DIR, subj)\n        sessions = sorted(d for d in os.listdir(subj_dir)\n                          if os.path.isdir(os.path.join(subj_dir, d)))\n        for di, sess in enumerate(sessions):\n            dff, t, me, dt = load_session(os.path.join(subj_dir, sess))\n            bin_durations.append(dt * FRAMES_PER_BIN)\n\n            cls = discretize_quintiles(me)\n\n            n_bins = dff.shape[1]\n            n_trials = n_bins // BINS_PER_TRIAL\n            assert n_trials >= 2\n\n            ntr, ninp, nout = [], [], []\n            for k in range(n_trials):\n                sl = slice(k * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (k + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n                ntr.append(np.ascontiguousarray(dff[:, sl]))\n                ninp.append(t[sl][None, :].astype(np.float32))\n                nout.append(cls[sl][None, :])\n\n            neural.append(ntr)\n            inputs.append(ninp)\n            outputs.append(nout)\n            subject_idx.append(si)\n            brain_region_idx.append(np.zeros(dff.shape[0], dtype=np.int64))\n            session_info.append({\n                'subject': subj,\n                'date': sess.split('_')[0],\n                'day_index': di,              # 0 = first recording day of this mouse\n                'n_neurons': int(dff.shape[0]),\n                'n_trials': int(n_trials),\n                'session_duration_s': float(t[-1]),\n                'motion_energy_quintile_edges': [\n                    float(v) for v in np.percentile(\n                        me, np.linspace(0, 100, N_OUTPUT_BINS + 1)[1:-1])],\n            })\n            print(f'{subj} {sess}: {dff.shape[0]} neurons, {n_trials} trials')\n\n    time_bin_size_ms = float(np.mean(bin_durations) * 1000.0)\n\n    data = {\n        'neural': neural,\n        'input': inputs,\n        'output': outputs,\n        'subjects': subjects,\n        'subject_idx': np.array(subject_idx, dtype=np.int64),\n        'brain_regions': [BRAIN_REGION],\n        'brain_region_idx': brain_region_idx,\n        'input_names': ['time_from_session_start_s'],\n        'output_names': ['motion_energy_quintile'],\n        'output_values': [['q1 (0-20%)', 'q2 (20-40%)', 'q3 (40-60%)',\n                           'q4 (60-80%)', 'q5 (80-100%)']],\n        'metadata': {\n            'task_description':\n                'Head-fixed neonatal mice (P7-P14) run spontaneously on a '\n                'non-motorised treadmill in the dark under sensory-minimised '\n                'conditions; there is no imposed task or stimulus. Decode the '\n                'animal\\'s movement (motion energy from behavioural videography, '\n                'discretised into five per-session equal-percentile bins) from '\n                'the activity of the same Track2p-tracked layer 2/3 barrel '\n                'cortex neurons.',\n            'time_bin_size': time_bin_size_ms,\n            'temporal_alignment_event':\n                'start of the 60 s trial block; each session is cut into '\n                'consecutive non-overlapping 1800-frame (60 s) blocks starting '\n                'at imaging onset',\n            'off_start': 0.0,\n            'off_end': float(np.mean(bin_durations) * BINS_PER_TRIAL),\n            'paper': 'Majnik et al. 2025, eLife 14:RP107540 '\n                     '(Track2p longitudinal tracking)',\n            'recording': 'two-photon calcium imaging (GCaMP8m), layer 2/3 of '\n                         'barrel cortex, 720x720 um FOV, ~30 Hz resonant scanner',\n            'neural_signal': 'baseline-corrected fluorescence (suite2p default '\n                             'maximin baseline, sigma=10 frames, 60 s window, no '\n                             'neuropil subtraction), averaged in bins of 10 frames',\n            'behavior_signal': 'global motion energy (sum of squared pixel-wise '\n                               'differences between consecutive video frames), '\n                               'realigned to the imaging frame grid via the camera '\n                               'timestamps, dropped frames linearly interpolated, '\n                               'averaged in bins of 10 frames',\n            'neuron_tracking': 'neurons are row-matched across all sessions of a '\n                               'given mouse (Track2p output); session order within '\n                               'a mouse is chronological',\n            'session_info': session_info,\n        },\n    }\n\n    with open(OUT_FILE, 'wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(data, f)\n    print(f'\\nwrote {OUT_FILE}: {len(neural)} sessions, '\n          f'{sum(len(s) for s in neural)} trials, '\n          f'time_bin_size {time_bin_size_ms:.2f} ms')\n\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    main()","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_017NB6uVQowpoDNnusmFKyhs","type":"tool_result","content":"We next applied Track2p to a longitudinal dataset consisting of daily recordings of the same 720×720 μm FOV throughout the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development (postnatal days 8 (P8) to P14, n=7 imaging sessions from one mouse; for more details see Methods.) In addition to the calcium indicator (GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic neurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice. This dual-labelling strategy facilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for Track2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3, for comparison with tracking based on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before running Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p for active cell detection (segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suite2p (ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\n\nWe next analyzed the development of functional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B for summary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 % std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. \n\nWe assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse movement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric.\n\nChronic 2-photon calcium imaging\n\nLongitudinal two-photon calcium imaging was performed for each mouse for at least 6 consecutive days (see Fig. 5 for details). Imaging was performed using a Bruker (Ultima 2P) microscope with a Coherent Mai-Tai excitation laser (950 nm excitation light) and a 16x Nikon objective (NA 0.8). Before detection, emitted light was split into two optical paths each associated with a filter (red and green, 580-620 and 500-550 nm respectively), allowing us to simultaneously record the GCaMP8m and the tdTomato signal. The acquisition was performed using the Prairie view software. All recordings were performed in layer 2/3 (depth between 100-200 µm from the pial surface) with a 720 × 720 µm field of view and 512 × 512 pixel resolution. Imaging rate was 30 Hz (resonant scanner) and each session lasted 20 minutes. All experiments were performed in the dark, under sensory-minimised conditions, with mice being free to spontaneously run on a non-motorised treadmill (Luigs and Neumann). To facilitate alignment and cell tracking, we kept the alignment of the head mount with respect to the microscope fixed across all sessions for the same mouse. To record from the same region across days we manually aligned the imaging plane in x, y and z to best match the reference images of the red channel (tdTomato) from all previous recording days. During the course of each recording, pups were kept warm by a heating element mounted to the imaging setup. After each imaging session, the pups were returned to their home cage with the dam and their littermates.\n\nVideography\n\nAll videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED light source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities.\n\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\n\nCalcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23 for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters).\nPreprocessing videography\n\nWe used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62 we quantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames in the videography recording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This yielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for all subsequent analyses.\n\nFunctional properties of tracked neurons\nCalcium event rates\n\nTo quantify the calcium event rate statistics we used SciPy’s peak detection algorithm (Python). For this we first denoised the traces slightly by averaging using a bin size of 10 frames and then proceeded to detect any peaks with a height and prominence of at least one standard deviation. We then calculated the rate as the number of peaks per minute within the recording. When quantifying the stability of the rates across days we computed the Pearson correlation coefficient across all neurons for each possible combination of sessions recorded from the same mouse.\n\nDecoding\n\nAll decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid overfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regression optimises the weights (β) that minimise the following loss function:\n\nL(\\beta) = \\|y - X\\beta\\|_2^2 + \\lambda\\|\\beta\\|_2^2\n\nWhere in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To choose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we used nested cross-validation (see for example 65,66), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting between training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by using the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to the model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is then evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter optimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this procedure for all possible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model evaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the inner and outer loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. For cross-day decoding we fit a new model with the optimal lambda for that given day and evaluated it on all other days. For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps. All models were implemented and fitted using PyTorch (Python).","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"720f23b5-b2b4-4453-8dd7-6c0019bc6abc","uuid":"9b048759-a8e6-456e-9c61-6328af0b76fb","timestamp":"2026-09-19T21:38:44.901Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"We next applied Track2p to a longitudinal dataset consisting of daily recordings of the same 720×720 μm FOV throughout the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development (postnatal days 8 (P8) to P14, n=7 imaging sessions from one mouse; for more details see Methods.) 'jm031/'):\n- For each subject there is a folder corresponding to the subject id\n- For cross-referencing with the paper the subjects are named in alphabeticaly increasing order (e. g. jm031 - mouse A, jm032 - mouse B ... jm046 - mouse F).\n\nsession sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a'):\n- Each subject folder contains a number of session folders, each corresponding to one recording day.\n- The name of the folder corresponds to the the recording date in the YYYY-MM-DD format (the '_a' in the end of the folder name can be ignored)\n- All recordings are done daily, starting between postnatal days P7 and P9 depending on the mouse (for more information see Fig5 B in the paper)\n\nsuite2p sub-sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p'):\n- Contains the neural data for the successfully tracked neurons for the session day in Suite2p format\n- This neural data is obtained by running Track2p using the 'save the outputs in suite2p format'\n- For more information on the Suite2p format see: https://suite2p.readthedocs.io/en/latest/outputs.html\n- For more information on the Suite2p format output of Track2p see: https://track2p.github.io/outputs_suite2p.html \n- Briefly the data only includes traces for the cells present across all days, meaning that the activity matrix (for example F.npy or spks.npy, are of shape n_neurons x n_tstamps) will have the same number of rows (neurons) each day and the rows will be matched (e. g. first row of matrix 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy' corresponds to the same neuron as the first row of the matrix on the next recording day 'jm031/2023-10-19_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy')\n- This data can also be visualised using the Suite2p GUI, for example by opening two recordings of the same mouse in two windows - in that case (thanks to the reindexing) the color code will be the same across the two days for the same neuron.\n\nmove_deve sub-sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/move_deve'):\n- Contains the processed behavioural data (motion energy extracted from videography of spontaneous behaviour 'motion_energy_glob.npy')\n- In some recordings there might be some missing frames from the camera (in case that length of 'motion_energy_glob.npy' doesn't match the number of 2-photon imaging frames)\n- The indices of missing frames can be obtained by looking at 'tstamps.npy' or 'interframe_int.npy' and treated as missing values for motion energy or they can be interpolated over\n\n\n## Other data availability:\n\n- Due to space constraints we could not provide the whole raw data\n- Upon reasonable request we can provide:\n    - This is the exact dataset the paper uses from Fig. 5 onward, so no further neuron/session/mouse curation was applied. All 6 mice × 6–7 days are kept.\n\n**Neural signal** — The paper: *\"baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters).\"* I reimplemented `track2p/gui/data_management.py::F_processing` exactly: `neucoeff=0` (no neuropil subtraction), maximin baseline (Gaussian σ=10 frames → min-filter → max-filter over a 60 s window), `dF = F − F_low`. Not z-scored, matching the paper (I tested z-scoring: validation accuracy 0.321 vs 0.301 — too small a gain to justify departing from the reference processing).\n\n**Alignment** — The camera was hardware-triggered by the microscope, so camera frame *i* = imaging frame *i*. Where the camera dropped frames (9 sessions, 1–148 frames), the gaps appear as exact 2× multiples of the frame interval in `interframe_int.npy`; I recover the true imaging-frame index from the cumulative gap count and verified it lands exactly on `nframes−1` in every affected session, then linearly interpolate the missing motion-energy values. Frame 0's motion energy is 0 by construction (no preceding frame) and is treated as missing. `tstamps.npy` is in units of 1000 s; ×1000 gives the true frame interval of 33.59 ms (29.78 Hz), used for the time input.\n\n**Binning / trials** — Both streams averaged in bins of 10 consecutive frames per the paper's decoding methods → `time_bin_size` = 335.88 ms. Trials are consecutive non-overlapping 1800-frame blocks (60 s at the nominal 30 Hz) = 180 bins; 20 trials per 20-min session, 30 per 30-min session.\n\n**Input/output** — Input is elapsed session time in seconds at each bin centre. Output is motion energy in 5 per-session quintile bins (exactly 20% per class, as intended).\n\n## Decoder result\n\n```\nTraining   balanced accuracy: 0.6019\nValidation balanced accuracy: 0.3007   (chance 0.2000)\n```\n\nModest but clearly above chance, consistent with the weak movement coupling in these P7–P14 animals — the correlation between mean population dF/F and the motion-energy quintile averages 0.12 across sessions (range −0.15 to 0.34).\nShell cwd was reset to /logs/verifier/judge/claude","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"720f23b5-b2b4-4453-8dd7-6c0019bc6abc","uuid":"b7c67608-c6f4-4cbc-9063-6cf762da8af2","timestamp":"2026-09-19T21:39:29.581Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"194 /tmp/traj.txt\n==================== 1 user\n[MSG] # Training a Neural Decoder\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository when applicable. All 6 mice × 6–7 days are kept.\n\n**Neural signal** — The paper: *\"baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters).\"* I reimplemented `track2p/gui/data_management.py::F_processing` exactly: `neucoeff=0` (no neuropil subtraction), maximin baseline (Gaussian σ=10 frames → min-filter → max-filter over a 60 s window), `dF = F − F_low`. Not z-scored, matching the paper (I tested z-scoring: validation accuracy 0.321 vs 0.301 — too small a gain to justify departing from the reference processing).\n\n**Alignment** — The camera was hardware-triggered by the microscope, so camera frame *i* = imaging frame *i*. Where the camera dropped frames (9 sessions, 1–148 frames), the gaps appear as exact 2× multiples of the frame interval in `interframe_int.npy`; I recover the true imaging-frame index from the cumulative gap count and verified it lands exactly on `nframes−1` in every affected session, then linearly interpolate the missing motion-energy values. Frame 0's motion energy is 0 by construction (no preceding frame) and is treated as missing. `tstamps.npy` is in units of 1000 s; ×1000 gives the true frame interval of 33.59 ms (29.78 Hz), used for the time input.\n\n**Binning / trials** — Both streams averaged in bins of 10 consecutive frames per the paper's decoding methods → `time_bin_size` = 335.88 ms. Trials are consecutive non-overlapping 1800-frame blocks (60 s at the nominal 30 Hz) = 180 bins; 20 trials per 20-min session, 30 per 30-min session.\n\n**Input/output** — Input is elapsed session time in seconds at each bin centre. Computation is in float64. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. Yes — both the neural traces and the motion-energy trace are rebinned by averaging non-overlapping blocks of **10 consecutive imaging frames**, taking ~29.8 Hz to ~2.98 Hz. The AI does not assume the nominal 30 Hz for the reported resolution: it measures the true inter-frame interval from the camera timestamps (median 33.59 ms → 29.78 Hz), multiplies by 10, and averages across the 41 sessions, reporting `time_bin_size = 335.88 ms`. Binning is applied to the continuous session *before* trial cutting and *before* the motion energy is discretised. The same `bin_mean` is applied to the timestamp vector so the time input refers to bin centres.\n\nii.\n```python\nFRAMES_PER_BIN = 10       # paper: denoise by averaging 10 consecutive timestamps\n\ndef bin_mean(x, k):\n    \"\"\"Average non-overlapping bins of k samples along the last axis.\"\"\"\n    n = (x.shape[-1] // k) * k\n    x = x[..., :n]\n    return x.reshape(*x.shape[:-1], n // k, k).mean(axis=-1)\n...\ndff_b = bin_mean(dff, FRAMES_PER_BIN)\nme_b  = bin_mean(me,  FRAMES_PER_BIN)\nt_b   = bin_mean(tstamps, FRAMES_PER_BIN)  # bin-centre time, seconds\nreturn dff_b.astype(np.float32), t_b, me_b, float(np.median(np.diff(tstamps)))\n...\nbin_durations.append(dt * FRAMES_PER_BIN)\ntime_bin_size_ms = float(np.mean(bin_durations) * 1000.0)\n```\n\niii. Using the recorded clock rather than `frame_index / 30` avoids a ~0.8 % drift that would accumulate to ~9 s of error by the end of a 20-min session.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from start of experiment*?\n\ni. Four steps: (1) unit conversion, `× 1000` to get seconds; (2) scatter onto the imaging-frame grid — the timestamps are written at the reconstructed imaging-frame indices `idx` and any imaging frame with no camera timestamp is left NaN; (3) `interp_nan` linearly interpolates those NaNs (edges filled with the nearest valid value); (4) `bin_mean` over 10 frames, giving the mean (bin-centre) time of each 335.9 ms bin. The vector is then sliced per trial and cast to float32 with shape `(1, 180)`. Time is **not** reset at trial boundaries — it increases monotonically across the whole session, so trial *k* starts near `60.46 × k` seconds.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interp_nan(x):\n    \"\"\"Linear interpolation over NaNs (edges filled with nearest valid value).\"\"\"\n    x = np.asarray(x, dtype=np.float64)\n    bad = np.isnan(x)\n    if bad.all():\n        raise ValueError('all values missing')\n    t = np.arange(len(x))\n    x[bad] = np.interp(t[bad], t[~bad], x[~bad])\n    return x\n```\n```python\ntstamps = np.full(n_frames, np.nan)\ntstamps[idx] = ts_cam\ntstamps = interp_nan(tstamps)\nt_b = bin_mean(tstamps, FRAMES_PER_BIN)\n...\nninp.append(t[sl][None, :].astype(np.float32))\n```\n\niii. The task spec says \"Time elapsed from the beginning of the session\", so the clock runs across trials rather than restarting. The scatter-and-interpolate step exists because the time vector must be defined on the *imaging* frame grid (the grid the neural data lives on), even at frames where the camera dropped out; the AI reuses exactly the same index map it built for the motion energy, which guarantees the two streams cannot drift apart. Binning with the same `bin_mean` that processes the neural data guarantees identical lengths.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from start of experiment* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. By construction, index-for-index. The timestamp vector is built at length `n_frames` (the number of *imaging* frames from `F.shape[1]`), not at the camera's length, so element *i* is the time of imaging frame *i*. It is then binned with the identical `bin_mean(·, 10)` call used for the neural traces and sliced with the identical `slice(k*180, (k+1)*180)`, so `input[s][k][0, j]` is the time of the bin in `neural[s][k][:, j]`. No interpolation or resampling between the two streams is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\nn_frames = F.shape[1]\n...\ntstamps = np.full(n_frames, np.nan)\ntstamps[idx] = ts_cam\ntstamps = interp_nan(tstamps)\ndff_b = bin_mean(dff, FRAMES_PER_BIN)\nt_b   = bin_mean(tstamps, FRAMES_PER_BIN)\n...\nsl = slice(k * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (k + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\nntr.append(np.ascontiguousarray(dff[:, sl]))\nninp.append(t[sl][None, :].astype(np.float32))\n```\n\niii. From the Methods: \"with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities.\" The AI restates this in its header (\"the behaviour video... was hardware-triggered by the microscope so that camera frame i == imaging frame i (up to dropped camera frames, which are recovered from the camera timestamps)\"), which is why placing the camera clock on the imaging grid is the correct alignment operation.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Motion energy* derived from?\n\ni. `move_deve/motion_energy_glob.npy` — the pre-computed scalar global motion energy per camera frame — together with `move_deve/tstamps.npy`, which is used to work out which imaging frame each motion-energy sample belongs to. The number of imaging frames, `F.shape[1]`, defines the target length. The AI does **not** use `interframe_int.npy` (the timestamp differences it computes itself with `np.diff` are the same quantity).\n\nii.\n```python\nme_cam = np.load(os.path.join(mov, 'motion_energy_glob.npy')).astype(np.float64)\n# tstamps are in units of 1000 s; convert to seconds\nts_cam = np.load(os.path.join(mov, 'tstamps.npy')).astype(np.float64) * 1000.0\nassert len(me_cam) == len(ts_cam)\n```\n\niii. The dataset README points at these files (\"Contains the processed behavioural data (motion energy extracted from videography of spontaneous behaviour 'motion_energy_glob.npy')... The indices of missing frames can be obtained by looking at 'tstamps.npy' or 'interframe_int.npy'\"). The AI's header records what the quantity is, from the Methods: \"the sum of squared pixel-wise differences between consecutive frames of the behaviour video\". The `assert len(me_cam) == len(ts_cam)` documents the assumption that the two camera-side arrays are in one-to-one correspondence.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Motion energy*?\n\ni. Five steps. (1) Reconstruct the imaging-frame index of every camera frame (see 4-d). (2) Scatter the motion energy into a full-length `n_frames` array of NaNs at those indices, so dropped frames are explicit NaNs. (3) Force `me[0] = NaN`: the first sample is identically 0 because motion energy is a frame *difference* and there is no preceding frame — the AI treats this as missing rather than as a genuine minimum. (4) `interp_nan` linearly interpolates all NaNs. (5) `bin_mean` over 10 frames, then `discretize_quintiles` (see 4-c). No smoothing, log transform, or cross-session normalisation is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\nidx = camera_frame_index(ts_cam, n_frames)\n\nme = np.full(n_frames, np.nan)\nme[idx] = me_cam\n# motion energy of the very first frame is 0 by construction (no preceding\n# frame to difference against): treat it as missing\nme[0] = np.nan\nme = interp_nan(me)\n...\nme_b = bin_mean(me, FRAMES_PER_BIN)\n...\ncls = discretize_quintiles(me)\n```\n\niii. The Methods describe the signal as already fully computed upstream (\"we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels\"), so the AI's job is only to put it on the imaging grid and denoise it. The 10-frame averaging follows the same Methods sentence used for the neural data (\"we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\"). The `me[0]` handling comes from the AI's inspection of the raw arrays in trajectory step 13, where it printed `me[:5]` and saw the leading zero; it is recorded in its summary as \"Frame 0's motion energy is 0 by construction (no preceding frame) and is treated as missing.\"\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Motion energy* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Into 5 equal-percentile bins (quintiles) with edges computed **independently for each session** on that session's binned motion-energy trace. `np.percentile` at 20/40/60/80 % gives four interior edges; `np.digitize` maps each sample to an integer label 0–4. Labels are stored as int64 with shape `(1, 180)` per trial, named `q1 (0-20%)` … `q5 (80-100%)`. The edges used are also saved per session in `metadata['session_info'][i]['motion_energy_quintile_edges']`. The class distribution comes out at exactly 0.200 for all five levels.\n\nii.\n```python\nN_OUTPUT_BINS = 5         # quintiles of motion energy\n\ndef discretize_quintiles(x, nbins=N_OUTPUT_BINS):\n    \"\"\"Assign each sample to one of `nbins` equal-percentile bins of x.\"\"\"\n    edges = np.percentile(x, np.linspace(0, 100, nbins + 1)[1:-1])\n    return np.digitize(x, edges).astype(np.int64)\n...\ncls = discretize_quintiles(me)\n...\n'output_names': ['motion_energy_quintile'],\n'output_values': [['q1 (0-20%)', 'q2 (20-40%)', 'q3 (40-60%)',\n                   'q4 (60-80%)', 'q5 (80-100%)']],\n...\n'motion_energy_quintile_edges': [\n    float(v) for v in np.percentile(\n        me, np.linspace(0, 100, N_OUTPUT_BINS + 1)[1:-1])],\n```\n\niii. Directly specified by the task: \"Motion energy, discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session.\" The AI restates it in the header: \"Output: motion energy discretised into 5 equal-percentile (quintile) bins, with the bin edges computed separately for each session\", and confirmed the result in the verification run (\"motion_energy_quintile: {q1 (0-20%) (0.200), ... q5 (80-100%) (0.200)}\"). Per-session edges are also the physically right choice here because absolute motion-energy scale depends on the day's camera/lighting geometry, and the paper itself treats motion energy as a within-session arousal proxy.\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The camera is hardware-triggered by the microscope, so nominally camera frame *i* is imaging frame *i*. When the camera drops frames the motion-energy array is shorter than the imaging data (9 of 41 sessions, by 1–148 frames). `camera_frame_index` recovers the true index: it takes the median inter-frame interval `dt`, computes `round(ifi / dt) - 1` missing frames for every gap, and cumulatively sums `1 + n_missing` to get each camera frame's imaging-frame index. Two assertions verify the reconstruction lands exactly on `n_frames - 1` and accounts for exactly the right number of missing frames. Motion energy is then scattered to those indices, the holes interpolated, binned, discretised, and sliced with the same `slice` object as the neural data — so alignment is index-for-index at every stage.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef camera_frame_index(tstamps, n_frames):\n    \"\"\"Map each recorded camera frame onto its imaging-frame index. ...\"\"\"\n    ncam = len(tstamps)\n    if ncam == n_frames:\n        # nothing dropped\n        return np.arange(n_frames)\n    ifi = np.diff(tstamps)\n    dt = np.median(ifi)\n    n_missing = np.round(ifi / dt).astype(int) - 1\n    idx = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(1 + n_missing)])\n    assert idx[-1] == n_frames - 1, 'could not reconstruct dropped camera frames'\n    assert n_missing.sum() == n_frames - ncam\n    return idx\n```\n```python\nme = np.full(n_frames, np.nan)\nme[idx] = me_cam\n...\nnout.append(cls[sl][None, :])\n```\n\niii. The Methods establish the synchronisation (\"the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition\"), and the README flags the failure mode (\"In some recordings there might be some missing frames from the camera... The indices of missing frames can be obtained by looking at 'tstamps.npy' or 'interframe_int.npy'... they can be interpolated over\"). The AI validated the reconstruction on the full dataset before adopting it (trajectory steps 27–31): it first tried `round(ts/dt)` directly, found it over-shot on three `jm046` sessions, and switched to the cumulative-gap formulation, then verified per session that `idx.max() == n_frames - 1` and `n_missing.sum()` matched the deficit everywhere. Its summary records the conclusion: \"the gaps appear as exact 2× multiples of the frame interval in `interframe_int.npy`; I recover the true imaging-frame index from the cumulative gap count and verified it lands exactly on `nframes−1` in every affected session\". One residual case is not handled: in `jm046/2024-09-05`, `2024-09-07` and `2024-09-08` the camera frame *count* equals `n_frames` yet the timestamps contain one internal gap of 3–10 intervals, so the `ncam == n_frames` short-circuit returns `arange` and leaves a sub-second shift after that point.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Three defects are handled explicitly. (1) **Dropped camera frames** (9 sessions): reconstructed from timestamp gaps and linearly interpolated (4-d), with two assertions guarding the reconstruction. (2) **The degenerate first motion-energy sample** (all sessions): `me[0]` is identically 0 because it has no preceding frame to difference against, so it is overwritten with NaN and interpolated from `me[1]`; otherwise it would be forced into the lowest quintile. (3) **Non-cell ROIs**: asserted absent rather than assumed. Interpolation is done by a shared `interp_nan` helper that raises if a trace is entirely missing and fills leading/trailing NaNs with the nearest valid value. There is no silent fallback anywhere: every anomaly either is repaired or raises. No remainder frames are discarded, because 36 000 and 54 000 are both exact multiples of 1800.\n\nii.\n```python\nassert len(me_cam) == len(ts_cam)\nassert np.all(iscell[:, 0] == 1), 'unexpected non-cell ROI in Track2p output'\nassert idx[-1] == n_frames - 1, 'could not reconstruct dropped camera frames'\nassert n_missing.sum() == n_frames - ncam\nassert n_trials >= 2\n```\n```python\ndef interp_nan(x):\n    x = np.asarray(x, dtype=np.float64)\n    bad = np.isnan(x)\n    if bad.all():\n        raise ValueError('all values missing')\n    t = np.arange(len(x))\n    x[bad] = np.interp(t[bad], t[~bad], x[~bad])\n    return x\n```\n\niii. The README sanctions interpolation (\"treated as missing values for motion energy or they can be interpolated over\"); the AI chose interpolation so that all trials keep a uniform 180 bins and no NaN reaches the decoder. Its summary explains the first-frame decision and the verification of the dropped-frame logic. The assert-heavy style is deliberate — the header claims the shipped data is already curated, and the assertions are what make that claim checkable rather than assumed.\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. `f_processing` dominates: on a 746 × 54 000 session it costs ~1.4 s, of which `gaussian_filter` over the time axis is ~0.93 s and the 1800-frame `minimum_filter1d`/`maximum_filter1d` pair ~0.4 s — roughly 1 s × 41 sessions. Two smaller costs: the `float64` up-cast of `F` and `Fneu` (two ~320 MB temporaries per session) plus the `F - 0.0 * Fneu` temporary, and the final `pickle.dump` of a 396 MB file. `np.load` of the `.npy` files is fast (<0.1 s/session). Total runtime is on the order of a minute, so nothing is a practical bottleneck.\n\nii.\n```python\nFlow = gaussian_filter(Fc, [0., sig_baseline])     # ~0.9 s per 746x54000 session\nFlow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, win)                 # win = 1800\nFlow = maximum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n...\ndff = f_processing(F.astype(np.float64), Fneu.astype(np.float64), fs)\n...\nwith open(OUT_FILE, 'wb') as f:\n    pickle.dump(data, f)\n```\n\niii. Not discussed in the trajectory — the AI never profiled or optimised, which is reasonable given the whole conversion finishes in about a minute (trajectory step 35 runs it in one shot with no complaint about runtime). The expense is intrinsic to the reference algorithm: the maximin baseline requires two sliding-window passes of length 1800 over every neuron's full trace.\n\n## 6-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Essentially none of substance. Every per-sample operation is already vectorised: `camera_frame_index` uses `np.diff`/`np.round`/`np.cumsum` instead of iterating over dropped frames, `interp_nan` uses a single `np.interp`, and `bin_mean` uses a reshape-and-mean. The only explicit loops are the subject loop, the session loop, and the per-trial slicing loop — 20–30 iterations of cheap `slice` views per session, where the `for k in range(n_trials)` body could in principle be replaced by a single reshape/`np.split`, but the gain would be unmeasurable and the explicit form is clearer. Notably, the AI avoided the obvious trap of inserting dropped frames one at a time with `np.insert` inside a Python loop (which reallocates the array on each call, and would also have been wrong for multi-frame gaps).\n\nii.\n```python\n# vectorised gap reconstruction instead of a per-drop insertion loop\nn_missing = np.round(ifi / dt).astype(int) - 1\nidx = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(1 + n_missing)])\n...\n# the only per-trial loop: 20-30 cheap slice views\nfor k in range(n_trials):\n    sl = slice(k * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (k + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n    ntr.append(np.ascontiguousarray(dff[:, sl]))\n```\n\niii. Not explicitly justified. The vectorised gap reconstruction was chosen for correctness rather than speed — the AI's exploration (trajectory step 31) showed it needed to count `round(ifi/dt) - 1` missing frames per gap, and that formulation is naturally array-based. The per-trial loop is retained because the target format is a list of per-trial arrays anyway.\n\n## 6-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. One genuine duplication: the quintile edges are computed twice per session — once inside `discretize_quintiles` to produce the labels, and again in the `session_info` dictionary literal to record them in metadata. The second `np.percentile` call is a redundant sort of the same ~5400-element array; the function could have returned its edges instead. Everything else is computed once per session and reused: `bin_mean` is called three times but on three different arrays, and the `idx` map from `camera_frame_index` is computed once and applied to both the motion energy and the timestamps rather than being rebuilt for each.\n\nii.\n```python\ncls = discretize_quintiles(me)          # computes np.percentile(me, [20,40,60,80]) internally\n...\n'motion_energy_quintile_edges': [\n    float(v) for v in np.percentile(\n        me, np.linspace(0, 100, N_OUTPUT_BINS + 1)[1:-1])],   # same call again\n```\n\niii. Not discussed. The repeat is a side effect of wanting the edges in `session_info` for provenance while keeping `discretize_quintiles` as a clean single-purpose helper with a scalar-array signature; the cost (41 extra percentile computations on ~5400 values) is negligible.\n\n## 6-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. Several small items, none costly except the float64 casts. (1) `Fneu.npy` is loaded, up-cast to float64 and multiplied by `neucoeff = 0.0` inside `f_processing` — a ~320 MB temporary and a full-array multiply whose result is always zero; the neuropil array is otherwise unused. (2) `iscell.npy` is loaded purely to feed an assertion. (3) `F` and `Fneu` are promoted to float64 for the whole pipeline, although the stored result is cast back to float32. (4) The timestamp vector is NaN-filled and interpolated at full 30 Hz resolution when only the 10-frame binned version is kept. (5) `np.ascontiguousarray` forces a copy of each neural trial slice, ~396 MB of duplication overall, where a view would serve (though the copy is what makes the pickle compact rather than pickling 41 whole-session arrays repeatedly). (6) Rich metadata the decoder never reads: `day_index`, `session_duration_s`, `motion_energy_quintile_edges`, `n_neurons`, `n_trials`, `paper`, `recording`, `neural_signal`, `behavior_signal`, `neuron_tracking`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef f_processing(F, Fneu, fs, neucoeff=0.0, ...):\n    Fc = F - neucoeff * Fneu        # neucoeff is always 0.0 -> Fneu has no effect\n...\ndff = f_processing(F.astype(np.float64), Fneu.astype(np.float64), fs)\niscell = np.load(os.path.join(s2p, 'iscell.npy'))   # only used by an assert\n...\ntstamps = np.full(n_frames, np.nan)\ntstamps[idx] = ts_cam\ntstamps = interp_nan(tstamps)                        # only t_b is kept\n...\nntr.append(np.ascontiguousarray(dff[:, sl]))\n```\n\niii. Not explicitly justified, but the shape of it is deliberate. The `Fneu`/`neucoeff=0.0` signature is kept because the AI's stated goal was to reproduce `track2p/gui/data_management.py::F_processing` *exactly*, including its parameter list, so that the departure from suite2p's 0.7 default is visible in the code rather than hidden; the same file in Track2p also loads `Fneu` and then does not use it. The `iscell` load backs the header's curation claim (\"assert rather than filter\"). The extra metadata is covered by the format spec's invitation to \"Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\".\nMDEOF\necho written; wc -l /logs/verifier/judge/claude/DECISIONS.md","description":"Write DECISIONS.md"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"container":null,"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":2,"cache_creation_input_tokens":332,"cache_read_input_tokens":69080,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":332,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":10,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"us"},"input_transformations":[],"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"720f23b5-b2b4-4453-8dd7-6c0019bc6abc","uuid":"bb51cc09-fcc4-4b4f-919a-17ac509c32e1","timestamp":"2026-09-19T21:44:01.032Z","request_id":"req_011CfDXMnm3MKgiTC3La1wsD","wire_tool_inputs":{"toolu_016szvQapHUrtUno9Zz9vH8o":{"command":"cat > /logs/verifier/judge/claude/DECISIONS.md << 'MDEOF'\n# Decisions\n\n## 1-a. How are the data split into subjects (mice)?\n\ni. One subject per top-level directory in `/app/data`, sorted alphabetically, giving `['jm031','jm032','jm038','jm039','jm040','jm046']`.
